Critical

Compromised PyPI Package: mrmustard 0.7.4 Steals SSH, Cloud, and Kubernetes Credentials

StepSecurity
2026-07-25 12 use cases 4 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

mrmustard 0.7.4 on PyPI was a credential stealer that ran on import, exfiltrating SSH keys, AWS, and Kubernetes credentials. Here is the analysis, IOCs, and how to respond.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Malicious mrmustard 0.7.4 artifact by hash or filename
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Compromised PyPI Package: mrmustard 0.7.4 Steals SSH, Cloud, and Kubernetes Cred
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] mrmustard persistence artifacts: mmcompat.pth and .tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc · [LLM] Recurring cron/shell-rc execution of .tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc payload · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] mrmustard stealer C2 exfil to metrics.femboy.energy · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Python interpreter reading SSH + AWS + Kube credential stores · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
mrmustard stealer C2 exfil to metrics.femboy.energy AICommand & Control High
Hunts outbound connections/DNS to the attacker-controlled domain femboy.energy (XOR-decoded C2 https://metrics.femboy.energy/v1/collect) used by the trojanised mrmustard 0.7.4 PyPI package to exfiltrate SSH keys, AWS and Kubernetes credentials. The domain is a hard IOC confirmed by two independent vendor writeups, so any resolution or connection to it is high-fidelity. Rationale: Anchored on the hard C2 IOC metrics.femboy.energy / femboy.energy, confirmed by StepSecurity and safedep.io (XOR key tf_compat_v2 decodes to https://metrics.femboy.energy/v1/collect). Datadog left empty: the target hosts are Linux dev/HPC boxes and none of the listed Datadog sources carry Linux DNS/proxy telemetry. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/compromised-pypi-mrmustard-0-7-4-credential-stealer • https://safedep.io/mrmustard-malicious-pypi-package/
ATT&CKT1041 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel enrichment scanners or sandboxes deliberately resolving the IOC domain
  • Security researcher reproducing the malware in a lab that reports into production telemetry
  • DNS sinkhole / RPZ appliance logging the blocked query (which is actually a true positive being handled)
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the host and the initiating process (expect a python interpreter or the .cache/.tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc payload); confirm mrmustard 0.7.4 was installed. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block femboy.energy at DNS/proxy/firewall, and treat the machine as compromised. 3) Hunt: rotate every SSH key, AWS credential and kube config reachable from the host, then sweep the fleet for mrmustard 0.7.4, the mmcompat.pth file, the */15 cron and the shell-rc marker.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has "femboy.energy" or RemoteUrl endswith "femboy.energy"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
ImDnsActivity
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DnsQuery has "femboy.energy"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcIpAddr, DnsQuery, DnsResponseName, DstIpAddr, EventVendor, EventProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Python interpreter reading SSH + AWS + Kube credential stores AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts a single python process opening files across two or more of ~/.ssh, ~/.aws/credentials|config and ~/.kube/config in one burst — the exact harvesting behaviour of mrmustard 0.7.4's _check_tf_compatibility() stealer that runs on import. Correlating multiple distinct secret stores touched by one interpreter suppresses benign single-file reads. Rationale: Uses the exact credential-store paths the payload harvests (~/.ssh, ~/.aws/credentials, ~/.aws/config, ~/.kube/config, corroborated by safedep.io) and requires a python interpreter to touch >=2 distinct stores in one burst to cut benign single reads. Sigma omitted (aggregation/threshold, not single-event). Datadog omitted: listed linux.auditd schema exposes no file-path field. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/compromised-pypi-mrmustard-0-7-4-credential-stealer • https://safedep.io/mrmustard-malicious-pypi-package/
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1552.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • awscli / kubectl / ssh wrappers written in Python legitimately reading all three configs during a deploy
  • Ansible or Python IaC tooling enumerating cloud + cluster creds
  • Developer running a credential-audit or dotfile-sync script
  • conda/pip post-install hooks that read ~/.aws for private index auth
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "awscli" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "kubectl" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "ansible"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the InitiatingProcessCommandLine — a bare `python`/`python3 -c` or an import of mrmustard with no legitimate CLI wrapper is suspicious; check the host for mrmustard 0.7.4. 2) Contain: isolate and rotate all SSH/AWS/kube credentials the host could reach. 3) Hunt: correlate with outbound traffic to femboy.energy and with the mmcompat.pth / hw_probe.pyc persistence artifacts on the same host and across the fleet.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has "python"
| extend SecretStore = case(FolderPath has "/.ssh", "ssh", FolderPath has "/.aws", "aws", FolderPath has "/.kube", "kube", "")
| where SecretStore != ""
| summarize StoreCount = dcount(SecretStore), Stores = make_set(SecretStore), Files = make_set(FolderPath, 20), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessId, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| where StoreCount >= 2   // one python process touching >=2 of {ssh,aws,kube} = harvest pattern
| order by FirstSeen desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ActingProcessName has "python"
| extend SecretStore = case(TargetFilePath has "/.ssh", "ssh", TargetFilePath has "/.aws", "aws", TargetFilePath has "/.kube", "kube", "")
| where SecretStore != ""
| summarize StoreCount = dcount(SecretStore), Stores = make_set(SecretStore), Files = make_set(TargetFilePath, 20), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname, ActingProcessId, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| where StoreCount >= 2
| order by FirstSeen desc
#event_simpleName=FileOpenInfo ContextImageFileName=/python[0-9.]*$/i
| regex("/\\.(ssh/|aws/(credentials|config)|kube/config)", field=TargetFileName)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextProcessId], function=count(field=TargetFileName, distinct=true, as=SecretFiles))
| SecretFiles >= 2
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count dc(Filesystem.file_path) as distinct_paths values(Filesystem.file_path) as file_paths min(_time) as firstTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where (Filesystem.file_path="*/.ssh/*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*/.aws/credentials" OR Filesystem.file_path="*/.aws/config" OR Filesystem.file_path="*/.kube/config") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.user _time span=5m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| eventstats dc(eval(if(match(file_paths,"/\.ssh/"),"ssh",if(match(file_paths,"/\.aws/"),"aws",if(match(file_paths,"/\.kube/"),"kube",null()))))) as store_count by dest
| where distinct_paths >= 2
| convert ctime(firstTime)
| sort - firstTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count dc(Filesystem.file_path) as distinct_paths values(Filesystem.file_path) as file_paths min(_time) as firstTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where (Filesystem.file_path="*/.ssh/*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*/.aws/credentials" OR Filesystem.file_path="*/.aws/config" OR Filesystem.file_path="*/.kube/config") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.user _time span=5m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| eventstats dc(eval(if(match(file_paths,"/\.ssh/"),"ssh",if(match(file_paths,"/\.aws/"),"aws",if(match(file_paths,"/\.kube/"),"kube",null()))))) as store_count by dest
| where distinct_paths >= 2
| convert ctime(firstTime)
| sort - firstTime
mrmustard persistence artifacts: mmcompat.pth and .tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc AIInstallation High
Alerts on creation of the attacker's unique persistence files — the mmcompat.pth auto-run stub dropped into site-packages and the source-less bytecode payload ~/.cache/.tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc. Both filenames and the hidden .tf_cache directory are attacker-specific strings confirmed by safedep.io, so any write is high-fidelity. Rationale: mmcompat.pth, hw_probe.pyc and the hidden ~/.cache/.tf_cache/ directory are attacker-unique strings (safedep.io) with no legitimate counterpart, making file-create events on them near-zero-FP. Datadog omitted: no listed Linux file-write source. CloudWatch N/A (endpoint, not AWS). Cross-checked against: • https://safedep.io/mrmustard-malicious-pypi-package/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/compromised-pypi-mrmustard-0-7-4-credential-stealer
ATT&CKT1546 T1564.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate package genuinely named mmcompat shipping a .pth (none known — investigate any hit)
  • Backup/AV software rehydrating the malicious file into a quarantine or restore path
  • Analyst copying the sample onto a host for reversing
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the writing process is a python interpreter/pip and inspect mmcompat.pth contents (expect subprocess.Popen launching hw_probe.pyc). 2) Contain: isolate the host, delete mmcompat.pth, ~/.cache/.tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc, the */15 cron and shell-rc hook, and remove mrmustard 0.7.4. 3) Hunt: rotate all reachable credentials and sweep every dev/CI/HPC host with the same package for these three persistence artifacts.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileModified", "FileRenamed")
| where FileName =~ "mmcompat.pth" or FileName =~ "hw_probe.pyc" or FolderPath has "/.cache/.tf_cache/"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ActionType, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "mmcompat.pth" or TargetFileName =~ "hw_probe.pyc" or TargetFilePath has "/.cache/.tf_cache/"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, EventType
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime

| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Recurring cron/shell-rc execution of .tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc payload AIInstallation High
Alerts when python executes the hidden ~/.cache/.tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc payload, especially when spawned by cron (the */15 * * * * job) or a shell login (the ~/.bashrc/~/.zshrc hook marked '# tensorflow hardware compatibility check'). This catches the mrmustard stealer's re-execution long after the package is removed. Rationale: Keys on the exact recurring payload path ~/.cache/.tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc and its cron/shell-rc launchers (safedep.io: cron `*/15 * * * * /usr/bin/env python3 {} ...`, rc marker '# tensorflow hardware compatibility check'). Distinct pivot from the file-drop UC: process execution + parent (cron/shell) context and a 15-minute recurrence signature. Cross-checked against: • https://safedep.io/mrmustard-malicious-pypi-package/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/compromised-pypi-mrmustard-0-7-4-credential-stealer
ATT&CKT1053.003 T1546.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Unrelated tooling that happens to place a real hardware-probe under ~/.cache (verify the .tf_cache path and .pyc name)
  • Analyst re-detonating the sample in a monitored sandbox
  • Backup restore replaying historic process telemetry
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the parent is cron/crond or a login shell and that ~/.cache/.tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc exists. 2) Contain: isolate the host; `crontab -r`/remove the */15 entry, strip the '# tensorflow hardware compatibility check' block from ~/.bashrc/~/.zshrc/fish config, delete .tf_cache and mmcompat.pth. 3) Hunt: rotate reachable SSH/AWS/kube creds, correlate with femboy.energy egress, and sweep the fleet for the same cron/rc markers.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "hw_probe.pyc" or ProcessCommandLine has "/.cache/.tf_cache/"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "hw_probe.pyc" or ActingProcessCommandLine has "/.cache/.tf_cache/"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 8c2a4f6b-91d3-4e08-a5c7-3f1b7d9e0a44
description: Execution of the hidden .cache/.tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc bytecode payload dropped by trojanised mrmustard 0.7.4, typically re-launched via cron or shell rc hook
references:

  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'hw_probe.pyc'
      - '/.cache/.tf_cache/'
  condition: selection
level: high
source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:EXECVE (@process.command_line:*hw_probe.pyc* OR @process.command_line:*/.cache/.tf_cache/*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| CommandLine=/(hw_probe\.pyc|\/\.cache\/\.tf_cache\/)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count(as=execs))
ENDPOINT

| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process="*hw_probe.pyc*" OR Processes.process="*/.cache/.tf_cache/*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process="*hw_probe.pyc*" OR Processes.process="*/.cache/.tf_cache/*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Malicious mrmustard 0.7.4 artifact by hash or filename AIDelivery Medium
Alerts on the presence of the trojanised mrmustard 0.7.4 sdist/wheel on any host, matched by its confirmed SHA256 archive hashes or the mrmustard-0.7.4 filename. Version 0.7.3 and earlier and 1.0.0a pre-releases are clean; only 0.7.4 (which has no matching GitHub tag) is malicious. Rationale: Uses the two confirmed archive SHA256 hashes (sdist 0404f8..., wheel 81f0d1...) plus the mrmustard-0.7.4 filename — the only malicious version, cross-validated against the absent GitHub v0.7.4 tag. Falcon FileWritten carries no hash field so it matches on the archive filename; Defender/Sentinel add the hash because MDE hashes files it observes. Datadog omitted (no listed Linux file/hash source). Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/compromised-pypi-mrmustard-0-7-4-credential-stealer • https://safedep.io/mrmustard-malicious-pypi-package/
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A researcher/DFIR analyst intentionally downloading the sample for analysis
  • An internal PyPI mirror/artifact cache that pulled 0.7.4 before it was yanked
  • Filename-only match on an unrelated file coincidentally named mrmustard-0.7.4 (hash match disambiguates)
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the file was actually installed/imported (check for mmcompat.pth, .tf_cache/hw_probe.pyc, and femboy.energy egress) vs merely downloaded. 2) Contain: quarantine the artifact, isolate any host that imported it, and treat it as compromised. 3) Hunt: pip-uninstall mrmustard 0.7.4 everywhere, purge internal mirrors/caches of the version, and rotate all credentials reachable from affected hosts.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(90d)
| where SHA256 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5")
    or FileName startswith "mrmustard-0.7.4"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ActionType, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
| where TargetFileSHA256 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5")
    or TargetFileName startswith "mrmustard-0.7.4"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5") or SHA1 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5") or MD5 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5") or SHA1 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5") or MD5 in~ ("0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["0404f8590fdaef95280c1d908068f31bf2321fe887faabf0c2329ba67c7203cb", "81f0d1291a975d012d1b892cf9967557fdbb1ad4e1ac0545702ad235ace1cac5"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.process_name,
       Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
]

Pheditor forced-password-change auth bypass — POST to pheditor.php AIExploitation Medium
Hunts unauthenticated exploitation of GHSA-f25v-x6vr-962g: HTTP POST requests to pheditor.php that carry the password-change parameters, letting an attacker set an arbitrary admin password and obtain a session on any instance still on the default 'admin' password. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact endpoint filename and the three POST parameters named in the PoC. Rationale: Anchored on the literal endpoint 'pheditor.php' and POST method from the advisory PoC (curl -d 'pheditor_new_password=...'); cross-source confirmed the parameter names. POST-body params aren't in standard IIS/proxy logs, so this surfaces exposure + exploitation attempts by endpoint+method rather than body signature. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f25v-x6vr-962g • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/pheditor-terminal-command-allowlist-bypass-via-argument-injection-leads-to-rce-surviving-vector-after-6e108ffa755ed7ba • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-jvc5-6g7q-c843
ATT&CKT1190 T1556
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate admin logging in / changing the password through the Pheditor UI (also a POST to pheditor.php)
  • Uptime/synthetic monitors or link-checkers repeatedly hitting the editor URL
  • Authenticated DAST/vulnerability scans (Nessus, Burp, ZAP) in a maintenance window
  • First-run setup where the operator legitimately changes the default 'admin' password
  • CI/CD smoke tests that POST to the app
Suppression filter
// In Sentinel, restrict to externally-sourced IPs to cut trusted-admin noise:
// | where ipv4_is_private(cIP) == false
Response runbook
1) Triage: check whether cIP is external/untrusted and its reputation; pull all requests from that IP and look for a single POST that succeeded (302/200) with no preceding authenticated navigation — the bypass needs no prior login. 2) Contain: put Pheditor behind IP allow-listing or take it offline, force-reset the admin password to a non-default value, and upgrade composer:pheditor/pheditor to >= 2.0.8. 3) Hunt: from any suspect host, look for newly-created .php files under the webroot (UC2), terminal/command actions via pheditor, and outbound connections from the web-server process indicating a planted webshell.

| order by Requests desc

Web-server / PHP process writes new .php file under webroot (post-Pheditor webshell drop) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the downstream impact of the Pheditor auth bypass: once an attacker holds an admin session, Pheditor's save/newfile action allows arbitrary PHP file creation, so the web-server or PHP interpreter process writes a new .php file into the webroot — a classic webshell drop. Article-specific because Pheditor explicitly documents PHP file creation/editing as core behaviour reachable post-bypass. Rationale: Uses the advisory's own scope note that Pheditor's save/newfile action creates arbitrary PHP files reachable after the bypass; pivots on the web-server/PHP interpreter creating .php under standard webroots (inetpub, htdocs, /var/www) — the concrete webshell-drop signal rather than a generic file write. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f25v-x6vr-962g • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/pheditor-terminal-command-allowlist-bypass-via-argument-injection-leads-to-rce-surviving-vector-after-6e108ffa755ed7ba
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate admin editing/creating PHP via Pheditor (inherent — PHP creation is Pheditor's documented core function)
  • Composer/dependency installs writing PHP into vendor directories
  • CMS plugin/theme or WordPress core updates dropping PHP files
  • Automated deployment pipelines syncing app code to the webroot
  • PHP opcache/session temp files or framework cache-warmers writing generated PHP
Suppression filter
// Defender: drop dependency-manager churn in vendor/cache trees
| where FolderPath !has "\\vendor\\" and FolderPath !has "/vendor/" and FolderPath !has "\\cache\\" and FolderPath !has "/cache/"
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the newly-written .php file's contents (or its hash) and check for webshell markers (eval, base64_decode, system, $_POST/$_GET passthrough); correlate the write time against UC1 POSTs to pheditor.php from the same host. 2) Contain: quarantine/remove the file, kill the web-server worker session, block the source IP, rotate the Pheditor admin password off the default, and patch to >= 2.0.8. 3) Hunt: enumerate all .php files created by the web-server process in the last 30 days, inspect outbound connections from that process for C2, and review web logs for post-drop GET/POST requests to the new filename.

[GHSA / CRITICAL] GHSA-p279-2cqp-84jg: OpenDJ SASL PLAIN authzid bypassing the proxy ACI scope check

OpenDJ SASL PLAIN authzid bypassing the proxy ACI scope check

ATT&CKT1190

OpenDJ SASL PLAIN bind invoking proxied authorization (authzid) — impersonation AIExploitation Medium
Hunts OpenDJ/OpenIdentityPlatform LDAP access-log BIND events where a SASL PLAIN bind supplies an authorization identity (authzid) that resolves to a DIFFERENT directory user than the authenticating bind DN. GHSA-p279-2cqp-84jg lets any proxied-auth holder assume any non-root identity without the mayProxy ACI grant, so a bind DN != authzid on the PLAIN path is the exact bypass signature. Rationale: Uses the article's exact bypass mechanic: SASL PLAIN bind (mechanism=PLAIN) carrying an authzid (dn:/u: forms) that resolves to a user other than the bind DN, with root/Directory Manager excluded per the advisory's 'Root not assumable' note. High-fidelity because legitimate proxied auth in a well-scoped deployment is rare and the bindDN!=authzid delta is the direct exploitation artifact. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p279-2cqp-84jg
ATT&CKT1078 T1134
Data sourcesAuthentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate middleware/service account (e.g. app connection pool) that is intentionally granted proxied-auth and a valid mayProxy ACI for target users
  • Directory sync/provisioning tooling that binds once and proxies as many end users
  • Migration or bulk-admin scripts using SASL PLAIN authzid within an authorized proxy scope
  • Test/staging OpenDJ instances where ACIs are deliberately permissive
  • Load-balancer or IdP broker performing proxied binds on behalf of authenticated sessions
Suppression filter
| where NOT (src_user in ("cn=svc-idbroker,ou=services,dc=corp,dc=com","cn=svc-provisioning,ou=services,dc=corp,dc=com"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: Pull the bind DN (src_user) and confirm whether it legitimately holds the proxied-auth privilege AND a mayProxy ACI covering the assumed authzid; if the ACI grant is absent, the bind exploited the flaw. 2) Contain: Revoke or restrict the proxied-auth privilege on that account (advisory workaround) and force-rotate its credentials; upgrade opendj-server-legacy above 5.1.1. 3) Hunt: Enumerate every distinct authzid that bind DN assumed, correlate downstream LDAP modify/search actions performed under those identities, and review whether any privileged (non-root) accounts were impersonated.
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ProcessName has "opendj" or SyslogMessage has "OpenDJ" or SyslogMessage has "SASL"
| where SyslogMessage has "PLAIN" and SyslogMessage has "authzid"
| extend BindDN = extract(@'(?i)bindDN="?([^",]+)', 1, SyslogMessage)
| extend AuthzID = extract(@'(?i)authzid="?(?:dn:|u:)?([^",]+)', 1, SyslogMessage)
| where isnotempty(AuthzID) and tolower(BindDN) != tolower(AuthzID)
| where AuthzID !contains "cn=directory manager"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, HostIP, BindDN, AuthzID, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc

OpenDJ proxied-auth fan-out — one source assuming many distinct authz identities AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts a single OpenDJ bind DN that assumes many DISTINCT authzid identities via SASL PLAIN within a short window — the abuse pattern of GHSA-p279-2cqp-84jg where a proxied-auth holder walks across arbitrary resolvable users beyond its intended ACI scope. A legit proxy account usually impersonates a stable, small set; broad fan-out is the escalation signal. Rationale: Builds on the article's core claim that a proxied-auth holder can assume ANY resolvable non-root identity: a distinct-authzid count >=5 per bind DN in a 10-minute window separates deliberate identity-walking from a legitimate service that proxies a stable small set. Threshold + temporal correlation, hence hunting-tier and no single-event Sigma. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p279-2cqp-84jg
ATT&CKT1078 T1134
Data sourcesAuthentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IdP/SSO broker binding once and proxying as each authenticated end user (naturally high distinct authzid count)
  • Directory sync or reconciliation jobs that iterate many users under an authorized proxy ACI
  • Reporting/compliance tooling enumerating per-user views via proxied auth
  • Connection-pooled middleware serving many customers from one service bind DN
  • Bulk password-reset or onboarding automation running under a sanctioned proxy account
Suppression filter
| where NOT (src_user in ("cn=svc-idbroker,ou=services,dc=corp,dc=com","cn=svc-provisioning,ou=services,dc=corp,dc=com"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: Identify the fan-out bind DN, list the distinct authzids it assumed, and confirm whether a mayProxy ACI actually scopes it to those targets; unscoped breadth = exploitation. 2) Contain: Restrict/revoke the proxied-auth privilege on that account, rotate its credentials, and prioritize upgrading opendj-server-legacy past 5.1.1 across the directory tier. 3) Hunt: For each impersonated identity, pull the LDAP operations (search/modify/add) executed under it, flag any that touched privileged groups or password attributes, and check other bind DNs holding proxied-auth for the same fan-out shape.
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where SyslogMessage has "PLAIN" and SyslogMessage has "authzid"
| extend BindDN = extract(@'(?i)bindDN="?([^",]+)', 1, SyslogMessage)
| extend AuthzID = extract(@'(?i)authzid="?(?:dn:|u:)?([^",]+)', 1, SyslogMessage)
| where isnotempty(AuthzID) and tolower(BindDN) != tolower(AuthzID)
| where AuthzID !contains "cn=directory manager"
| summarize DistinctAuthzIDs = dcount(AuthzID), AssumedIdentities = make_set(AuthzID, 25), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by BindDN, Computer, bin(TimeGenerated, 10m)
| where DistinctAuthzIDs >= 5
| order by DistinctAuthzIDs desc
source:linux.syslog opendj PLAIN authzid
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app="opendj" by Authentication.src_user, Authentication.user, _time span=10m 
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)` 
| where lower(src_user)!=lower(user) AND NOT like(lower(user), "%cn=directory manager%") 
| stats dc(user) as distinct_authzids values(user) as assumed_identities min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime by src_user 
| where distinct_authzids >= 5 
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)`

Budibase: Unauthenticated REST Datasource Credential Theft via Cross-Origin Auth Leak

Budibase REST query published to PUBLIC role (unauth cred-leak enabler) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the specific enabling step of GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5: an attacker grants the PUBLIC role to a Budibase REST-datasource query (POST /api/permission/PUBLIC/query_.../read|write) so it becomes executable with no session. This is the pivot that turns a stored-credential leak into a fully unauthenticated one-request theft. Rationale: Uses the exact PoC path /api/permission/PUBLIC/query_<id>/{read,write} (article Line 3). Requiring the 'query_' resource prefix scopes to REST-query publishing (the vuln's precondition) rather than generic public-app tables. Cross-checked GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5 and its predecessor CVE-2026-48152/GHSA-3gp5. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/CVE-2026-48152/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A Budibase developer legitimately publishing an intended public app that includes a public REST query
  • Staging/QA tenants where public apps are the norm and PUBLIC grants are frequent
  • App-deployment/CI pipelines re-applying resource permissions during promotion
  • Budibase version upgrade/migration routines re-writing permission documents
  • Builder UI bulk permission changes when many resources are set PUBLIC at once
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the query id from the PUBLIC-grant URL and identify its datasource via GET /api/datasources; confirm whether it is a REST datasource holding Bearer/Basic/static-header authConfigs and whether the grant was expected. 2) Contain: revoke PUBLIC on the query (set role back to BASIC/POWER) or unpublish the app; rotate any REST datasource credentials attached to that query as potentially exposed. 3) Hunt: search web/egress logs for subsequent anonymous POST /api/v2/queries/<id> from external IPs and for undici-originated outbound requests to non-datasource hosts (see companion UC) to confirm whether credentials were actually exfiltrated.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod =~ "POST"
| where Url has "/api/permission/PUBLIC/query_"          // granting PUBLIC role specifically to a *query* resource
| where Url endswith "/read" or Url endswith "/write"
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), Grants=make_set(Url, 25), Count=count()
         by SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, HttpUserAgent=HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, HttpStatusCode
| order by FirstSeen desc

description: Detects a Budibase permission grant that makes a REST datasource query executable by unauthenticated PUBLIC users, the enabling step for GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5 cross-origin credential theft.

Budibase server leaks datasource auth to first-seen host (undici + Authorization egress) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the actual credential exfiltration of GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5: the Budibase server's server-side fetch (User-Agent 'undici') sends a stored REST datasource Authorization/Bearer header to a destination host it has never contacted before — the attacker-chosen server reached via an absolute-URL {{ parameter }} query path. Because the same undici+Authorization shape is used for legitimate datasource calls, the tell is a first-seen destination host. Rationale: Pulls the two article-specific artifacts straight from the Line-4 response: User-Agent 'undici' and an attached 'authorization: Bearer' header being delivered to an arbitrary host (postman-echo.com in the PoC). Legitimate datasource calls share this shape, so a first-seen/leftanti destination host isolates the cross-origin leak. Defender/Falcon/CloudWatch left empty — the discriminator is outbound HTTP headers, which endpoint EDR and CloudTrail do not carry. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/GHSA-mqhr-6j6h-74p5/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/server-budibase-unauthenticated-rest-datasource-credential-theft-via-cross-origin-auth-leak-b3289fddaf47b17b
ATT&CKT1552 T1567
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A newly configured legitimate REST datasource pointing at a genuinely new external API (benign first-seen host)
  • Budibase calling a third-party SaaS API endpoint that was recently added or changed
  • API provider migrating to a new hostname / regional failover changing the datasource base host
  • Proxy log gaps or retention rollover making an established host appear 'first-seen'
  • Datasource that legitimately uses templated/dynamic paths spanning multiple partner hosts
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the first-seen DstHostname and whether it maps to any configured Budibase REST datasource; treat unknown/attacker-looking hosts (webhook.site, postman-echo.com, requestbin, raw IPs) as high priority. 2) Contain: block egress to the destination host at the proxy/firewall and immediately rotate the Bearer/Basic tokens and static header secrets for every REST datasource whose credentials could have been attached. 3) Hunt: pivot back to web access logs for the anonymous POST /api/v2/queries/<id> that triggered it and to the companion UC for the preceding PUBLIC-role grant; enumerate all REST datasources on the tenant and rotate any with high-value stored auth.
let lookback = 14d;
let baseline = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated between (ago(lookback) .. ago(1d))
    | where HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has "undici"          // Budibase server-side fetch UA
    | summarize by DstHostname;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has "undici"
| where isnotempty(HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization)          // stored datasource credential is being attached
| join kind=leftanti baseline on DstHostname               // destination host never before contacted by undici = candidate leak target
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, HttpHostName, Url, HttpMethod, HttpUserAgent=HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Budibase OIDC login sequence (configs → callback → self) from single source AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the exact 3-request OIDC flow the PoC drives to exploit CVE-class GHSA-hp6v-6jw7-gv2f: a client hits the Budibase provider-config endpoint, returns to the OIDC callback, then immediately queries /api/global/self to confirm the identity it landed on. Article-specific because it keys on Budibase's precise auth endpoints rather than generic SSO. Rationale: Uses the article's exact Budibase auth endpoints (/api/global/auth/<provider>/oidc/configs/, /api/global/auth/oidc/callback, /api/global/self) that the PoC walks in order; the three-step correlation from a single source is far higher-fidelity than watching the callback alone. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/GHSA-hp6v-6jw7-gv2f/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/server-budibase-oidc-sso-account-takeover-incoming-identity-linked-by-email-without-checking-5957d62fa1838118 • https://github.com/coder/coder/security/advisories/GHSA-75vm-6w67-gwvp
ATT&CKT1078.004 T1550
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate first-time OIDC enrolment of a real user who then loads their own profile
  • Health-check / synthetic monitoring that scripts the full login flow
  • Automated SSO integration tests in a staging Budibase instance
  • A single NAT/proxy egress IP aggregating many genuine users so multiple flows collapse onto one src
  • Admin manually reconfiguring the OIDC provider and re-testing login
Suppression filter
| where DstHostname has_any ("budibase") // scope to Budibase hosts; then exclude known monitoring source IPs and staging hosts via a watchlist join
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the Budibase app log / DB user record for the account the session resolved to — check whether it is a pre-existing local (password) admin now showing providerType=oidc and whether the IdP sub differs from any prior link. 2) Contain: revoke the budibase:auth session, force-logout the account, and disable the affected OIDC provider config until email_verified enforcement is confirmed; reset the victim admin. 3) Hunt: enumerate all accounts whose providerType flipped to oidc without a matching prior sub, and review the trusted IdP (Keycloak/Authentik) for self-registrations or profile email edges asserting internal addresses with email_verified=false.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where Url has_any ("/oidc/configs/", "/api/global/auth/oidc/callback", "/api/global/self")
| extend step = case(Url has "/oidc/configs/", "1_init", Url has "/oidc/callback", "2_callback", Url has "/api/global/self", "3_self", "other")
| where step != "other"
| summarize steps = dcount(step), stepsSeen = make_set(step), firstTime = min(TimeGenerated), lastTime = max(TimeGenerated), reqs = count() by SrcIpAddr, DstHostname
| where steps >= 3   // all three OIDC-takeover stages seen from one source
| order by firstTime desc

IdP self-registration or profile email-change asserting a privileged Budibase user's email AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the Budibase-side effect of the merge bug: an OIDC callback from a source that then exercises global-admin/builder-only endpoints (/api/global/users, /api/global/roles, /api/global/configs) — i.e. a freshly-SSO-authenticated session immediately wielding privileged APIs, the tell-tale of inheriting a victim admin's roles via an unverified email claim. ATT&CKT1078.004 T1548
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A genuine SSO-provisioned admin logging in and doing normal user/role administration
  • Shared egress IP where an admin's real session and other users' logins collapse onto one src

let LookbackDays = 7d;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where Url has_any ("/api/global/auth/oidc/callback", "/api/global/users", "/api/global/roles", "/api/global/configs")

| extend isAdmin = iif(Url has "/api/global/users" or Url has "/api/global/roles" or Url has "/api/global/configs", 1, 0)

| where callbacks >= 1 and adminCalls >= 1   // OIDC login then admin-only API use from same source
| order by firstTime desc

Stacked SQL-injection payload against Budibase datasource query API (multipleStatements) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts inbound HTTP requests to Budibase's datasource/query REST endpoints carrying stacked SQL (a semicolon-separated second statement) with destructive/exfil keywords such as DROP TABLE, DELETE, GRANT ALL or INTO OUTFILE. This is article-specific because the vuln (GHSA-q6x4-v3qx-85qw) is that @budibase/server <=3.38.1 sets multipleStatements:true, so a single injected `;` executes a second attacker-chosen statement. Rationale: Uses the two invariant tokens of this vuln: the Budibase query API path (/api/queries, /api/datasources) and the stacked-statement pattern (`;` + DROP TABLE / INTO OUTFILE / GRANT ALL) that multipleStatements:true uniquely allows. Cross-checked against the GitLab advisory mirror and OffSeq radar entry. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/GHSA-q6x4-v3qx-85qw/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/server-budibase-sql-injection-via-multiplestatements-true-3b00631cb39a1028 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/budibase/GHSA-qqf5-x7mj-v43p/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security scanners / DAST tools (Burp, sqlmap, Nessus) probing the Budibase instance in an authorized pentest
  • Legitimate Budibase query builder where an admin genuinely writes a query containing the word DELETE or DROP as part of app logic
  • WAF learning/tuning mode replaying captured payloads
  • A user field legitimately containing text like 'grant all access' being URL-encoded into the request
  • Automated regression tests that exercise datasource CRUD endpoints
Suppression filter
| where not(SourceIP in ("10.0.0.0/8")) // exclude sanctioned internal scanner ranges; replace with your DAST subnet
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full request from the WAF/proxy log and confirm the second statement after `;` is attacker-controlled (DROP/DELETE/OUTFILE/GRANT) rather than app-legitimate text; note source IP, auth state and whether it hit /api/queries/preview. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF, disable the affected MySQL datasource in Budibase, and upgrade @budibase/server past 3.38.1 (or hot-patch multipleStatements:false in packages/server/src/integrations/mysql.ts). 3) Hunt: review MySQL general/audit logs for stacked statements from the Budibase DB user, and run UC-2 to check whether mysqld wrote any INTO OUTFILE dumps to /tmp or the web root.

union isfuzzy=true

MySQL server (mysqld) writes exfil file via SELECT INTO OUTFILE on Budibase DB host AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the mysqld process creating files outside its data directory — the server-side footprint of a successful SELECT INTO OUTFILE data-theft chain that the Budibase multipleStatements SQL injection enables. Article-specific because the advisory explicitly lists 'Exfiltrate data via SELECT INTO OUTFILE' as an impact, and mysqld writing to /tmp, a web root, or Temp is anomalous for a normal DB workload. Rationale: Pivots on the advisory's named exfil primitive (SELECT INTO OUTFILE) by detecting mysqld itself creating files outside /var/lib/mysql - a behaviour that normal Budibase-driven MySQL workloads never exhibit. Excludes the datadir to keep the signal clean. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/GHSA-q6x4-v3qx-85qw/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/server-budibase-sql-injection-via-multiplestatements-true-3b00631cb39a1028
ATT&CKT1190 T1005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • DBA using SELECT INTO OUTFILE for a legitimate scheduled data export to a reporting directory
  • mysqldump or backup tooling writing dumps to /tmp or a staging path
  • MySQL error/slow-query logs configured to write outside the datadir
  • Application code that legitimately exports CSVs via the DB engine
  • Migration jobs writing .sql files during a maintenance window
Suppression filter
| where not(FolderPath has_any ("/var/backups/", "/opt/mysql-exports/")) // exclude sanctioned DBA export/backup directories
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the created file, open it read-only to confirm it contains dumped table rows, and correlate its creation time against UC-1 WAF hits / the Budibase datasource user's MySQL query log. 2) Contain: preserve then remove the exfil file, disable the Budibase MySQL datasource, set secure_file_priv to a locked-down path (or NULL to disable OUTFILE), and revoke FILE privilege from the Budibase DB account. 3) Hunt: search web-server and proxy egress for retrieval of the written file, review MySQL logs for other stacked statements from the same session, and confirm no DROP/DELETE (data destruction) statements executed.

| where not(FolderPath has_any ("/var/lib/mysql/", @"\MySQL\", @"\Data\"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessId, FolderPath, FileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| sort by Timestamp desc

title: MySQL Server Writes File Outside Data Directory (SELECT INTO OUTFILE Exfil)

  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-q6x4-v3qx-85qw

  category: file_event

    Image|endswith:
      - '/mysqld'

| sort(count, order=desc)

OpenAM /authservice CustomCallback ClassName XML — CVE-2026-62379 pre-auth Class.forName RCE AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound PLL remote-auth requests to OpenAM's /authservice endpoint whose XML body carries a <CustomCallback>/<ClassName> element — the exact vector CVE-2026-62379 abuses to make the server Class.forName-load and instantiate an attacker-named Java class without authentication. Article-specific because it keys on the two OpenAM remote-auth DTD tags that drive the vulnerable code path, not generic 'suspicious POST'. Rationale: Keys on the concrete OpenAM PLL remote-auth tokens `/authservice` + `<CustomCallback>`/`<ClassName>` that AuthXMLUtils.createCustomCallback passes to Class.forName (per the GHSA and OffSeq/GitLab write-ups); a POST to that endpoint carrying those XML tags is the exploit itself, not a generic probe. Body match requires a proxy/WAF that logs request payloads. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wg5r-wc3x-39vc • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/orgopenidentityplatformopenamopenam-core-openam-unauthenticated-remote-code-execution-via-classforname-7678e3fe5efb8e5c • https://advisories.gitlab.com/maven/org.openidentityplatform.openam/openam-scripting/CVE-2026-47424/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimately configured distributed-auth UI or policy agents that POST CustomCallback XML to /authservice when a site genuinely uses custom callback modules
  • Vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Qualys/Nuclei) probing the endpoint post-disclosure
  • Internal red-team / patch-validation testing of CVE-2026-62379
  • Load-balancer or uptime health checks hitting /authservice (these lack the CustomCallback/ClassName body, so the body filter suppresses them)
Suppression filter
| where not (RequestClientApplication has_any ("Nessus","Qualys","Nuclei","nikto") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full request body for the matched event; inspect the <ClassName> value — a JDK/attacker-controlled class (e.g. anything not an OpenAM callback package) plus an external/unauthenticated SourceIP is a true positive. Confirm the target OpenAM build is <=16.1.1. 2) Contain: block the SourceIP at the WAF, enable sunRemoteAuthSecurityEnabled to force the remote-auth security token, and restrict external access to /authservice; schedule the 16.1.2 upgrade. 3) Hunt: pivot to the OpenAM host and check for java/tomcat spawning shells (UC2), new .jsp files under webapps, and outbound connections from the app-server user in the minutes following the request.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestURL has "/authservice"
| where RequestMethod =~ "POST"
// <CustomCallback>/<ClassName> are the OpenAM remote-auth DTD tags Class.forName consumes
| where Message has_any ("CustomCallback","ClassName") or RequestContext has_any ("CustomCallback","ClassName") or DeviceCustomString1 has_any ("CustomCallback","ClassName") or DeviceCustomString2 has_any ("CustomCallback","ClassName")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestMethod, RequestClientApplication, DeviceProduct, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc

OpenAM/Tomcat java process spawning shell or LOLBin — CVE-2026-62379 post-exploitation AIInstallation High
Detects the OpenAM app-server JVM (java/javaw, Tomcat/Catalina) spawning an interactive shell or download/recon LOLBin — the immediate on-host consequence of CVE-2026-62379 instantiating attacker code via Class.forName. Scoped to OpenAM by requiring openam/catalina/tomcat/forgerock in the parent command line so it isn't a generic 'java spawns cmd' rule. Rationale: OpenAM is a Java/Tomcat app, so successful CVE-2026-62379 code execution surfaces as the JVM parenting a shell/LOLBin; anchoring the parent command line to openam/catalina/tomcat/forgerock keeps it OpenAM-specific and strips generic java build-tool noise. Cross-checked against the GHSA advisory and OffSeq threat radar entry describing full-server code execution. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wg5r-wc3x-39vc • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/orgopenidentityplatformopenamopenam-core-openam-unauthenticated-remote-code-execution-via-classforname-7678e3fe5efb8e5c
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.003 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • OpenAM/Tomcat lifecycle scripts (catalina.sh start/stop) that legitimately fork a shell during startup or shutdown
  • ssoadm / amster CLI or backup cron jobs invoked through the app-server user that shell out
  • Monitoring/APM agents embedded in the JVM that exec helper binaries (e.g. curl for health probes)
  • Admin running diagnostic commands (whoami/net) under the tomcat service account during maintenance
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has_any ("catalina.sh start","catalina.sh stop","catalina.sh run","shutdown","ssoadm","amster")
Response runbook
1) Triage: examine the child ProcessCommandLine — recon (whoami/id/net), downloaders (curl/wget/certutil to external hosts), or reverse-shell one-liners under the tomcat/openam service account are true positives; benign catalina start/stop is not. Correlate the timestamp against UC1 /authservice hits on the same host. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the offending process tree, rotate OpenAM service-account and datastore/admin credentials, and enable sunRemoteAuthSecurityEnabled while scheduling the 16.1.2 upgrade. 3) Hunt: look for dropped .jsp/.class webshells under Tomcat webapps, new cron/systemd or scheduled-task persistence created by the service user, and outbound C2 from the JVM.

OpenAM WebAuthn pre-auth Java deserialization payload to /json/authenticate (CVE-2026-62263) AIExploitation High
Hunts inbound HTTP requests to OpenAM's WebAuthn authentication endpoint carrying a base64-encoded Java serialized object (magic bytes AC ED 00 05 => 'rO0AB'). The vuln's ObjectInputFilter allows the AuthenticatorImpl root but leaves the nested gadget graph unchecked, so the attacker delivers the gadget chain inside a serialized authenticator via an attacker-chosen userHandle — pre-auth. A serialized-object marker on the authenticate endpoint is near-unambiguous exploitation. Rationale: Uses the article's exact vuln surface: OpenAM WebAuthn authenticator deserialization reached pre-auth via userHandle on the /json/authenticate endpoint. The 'rO0AB' token is the base64 of the Java serialization magic (0xAC 0xED 0x00 0x05) named implicitly by the deserialization sink; its presence on the authenticate endpoint is exploitation, not normal WebAuthn traffic (which sends base64url userHandle, not serialized graphs). defender_kql/falcon/datadog/cloudwatch left empty: this is server-side web-payload telemetry that lives on a WAF/reverse proxy, not Defender AH, FDR, or AWS. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-gf8h-gq53-288j • https://advisories.gitlab.com/maven/org.openidentityplatform.openam/openam-auth-webauthn/CVE-2026-45051/ • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/ghsa-6c99-87fr-6q7r • https://github.com/OpenIdentityPlatform/OpenAM/wiki/How-to-Setup-WebAuthn-Authentication-in-OpenAM
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized pentest / red-team probing OpenAM with ysoserial or Nuclei deserialization templates (true positive, confirm authorization)
  • Internal vulnerability scanner (Tenable/Qualys) sending crafted deserialization test payloads to the auth endpoint
  • Legitimate WebAuthn logins — these send base64url userHandle values and will NOT contain the 'rO0AB' serialized marker, so they do not match
  • WAF that base64-encodes and re-logs an entire request in a field that happens to already contain a serialized cookie from a different app
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Pull the full request from the WAF/proxy log (RequestContext/Message). Confirm the payload after 'rO0AB' is a serialized graph and identify the SourceIP, whether the request got HTTP 200/500, and if it was pre-auth (no valid session). 2) CONTAIN: Block the SourceIP at the WAF, take the exposed OpenAM node out of the LB pool, and confirm the org is on openam-auth-webauthn <=16.1.1 (vulnerable) — upgrade past 16.1.1. 3) HUNT: On the OpenAM host, run UC2 (java spawning shell children) for the same time window; review Tomcat/catalina logs for exceptions during readObject; check for new files, cron/systemd persistence, and outbound connections from the Java process.
// OpenAM WebAuthn deserialization RCE - base64 Java serialized object ('rO0AB' = 0xACED0005) to the authenticate endpoint
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestURL has_any ("/openam/json/authenticate", "/json/authenticate", "/openam/json/realms", "/json/realms")
| where RequestContext has "rO0AB" or Message has "rO0AB" or RequestURL has "rO0AB" or RequestCookies has "rO0AB"
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, DestinationHostName, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestContext, Message, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

OpenAM/Tomcat Java process spawning a shell — post-deserialization RCE (CVE-2026-62263) AIExploitation High
Hunts the OpenAM JVM (java/java.exe under Tomcat/catalina) spawning a command interpreter or download tool — the payload-execution stage after the WebAuthn gadget chain fires during readObject(). An IAM/SSO appliance's Java process spawning cmd/powershell/sh/curl is abnormal and high-fidelity for RCE. Rationale: Grounded in the article's sink (WebAuthn readObject/readResolve executes during readObject() before any assertion check) and the affected package org.openidentityplatform.openam. The parent-context filter (openam/catalina/tomcat/org.openidentityplatform in the JVM cmdline) pins this to the OpenAM appliance rather than any JVM, and pairing it with a shell/downloader child is the classic Java-deser RCE process shape. Cross-platform (java on Linux, java.exe on Windows) since OpenAM commonly runs on Tomcat/Linux. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-gf8h-gq53-288j • https://advisories.gitlab.com/maven/org.openidentityplatform.openam/openam-auth-webauthn/CVE-2026-45051/ • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/ghsa-6c99-87fr-6q7r
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1059.001 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Tomcat startup/shutdown wrappers where catalina.sh/java invokes 'sh -c' during normal service lifecycle
  • OpenAM plugin or scripted auth module that legitimately calls Runtime.exec for a health-check or LDAP helper script
  • Sysadmin running diagnostics under the tomcat/openam service account (whoami, curl to an internal health URL)
  • Monitoring/backup agent shelling out from the JVM for metrics collection
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("catalina.sh start","catalina.sh stop","catalina.sh run","-version","/usr/bin/id -u","/healthcheck","/monitoring/"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Inspect the child ProcessCommandLine — decode any base64/encoded command, and check whether it is a known startup script (catalina.sh) or a recon command (whoami, id, curl to attacker host). Correlate the timestamp against UC1 hits (serialized payload to /json/authenticate) on the same host. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the OpenAM host, kill the offending child and its descendants, and rotate OpenAM service-account and any credentials the JVM could read (LDAP bind, datastore, secrets). 3) HUNT: Search for webshells under the Tomcat webapps dir, new persistence (cron, systemd, Run keys), outbound C2 from the java process, and lateral auth from the OpenAM host; confirm the fix (openam-auth-webauthn > 16.1.1) is deployed everywhere.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("java.exe","javaw.exe","java")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("openam","catalina","tomcat","webauthn","org.openidentityplatform")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","bash","sh","dash","curl","curl.exe","wget","wget.exe","whoami","whoami.exe","python","python3","nc","ncat")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("java","java.exe","javaw.exe","catalina")
| where ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("openam","catalina","tomcat","webauthn","org.openidentityplatform")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell","pwsh","/bash","/sh","/dash","curl","wget","whoami","python","/nc","ncat")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Node.js host process spawning OS shell (Nunjucks SSTI child_process.execSync RCE) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts a Node.js runtime process spawning cmd.exe/powershell or a Unix shell, the direct result of an attacker-controlled .prompty template abusing @prompty/core's Nunjucks renderer to reach global.process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync(). This is article-specific because the RCE terminates in a node→shell parent/child chain that the vulnerable renderer produces. Rationale: Anchored on the corroborated Nunjucks RCE payload chain (range.constructor -> global.process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync) whose default execSync behaviour spawns cmd.exe /d /s /c on Windows and /bin/sh -c on Linux, producing a node->shell parent/child pair. Cross-checked PayloadsAllTheThings and HackTricks SSTI-JS references plus the GHSA advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-w28w-gp39-m4p6 • https://github.com/swisskyrepo/PayloadsAllTheThings/blob/master/Server%20Side%20Template%20Injection/JavaScript.md • https://hacktricks.wiki/en/pentesting-web/ssti-server-side-template-injection/index.html
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.003 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate Node build tooling (npm/yarn/webpack) invoking cmd.exe /c or /bin/sh -c for lifecycle scripts
  • Electron or CLI apps built on Node that shell out to system utilities by design
  • node-based process managers (pm2, nodemon) restarting apps via a shell
  • CI/CD runners executing shell steps under a Node orchestrator
  • Developer workstations running local server frameworks that exec git/openssl via child_process
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm-cli.js","yarn.js","webpack","nodemon","pm2","node-gyp") or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm run","prepare","postinstall","node_modules\\.bin"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full child command line and InitiatingProcessCommandLine — a benign build shows npm/yarn lifecycle strings; malicious shows ad-hoc recon (whoami, id, curl, /dev/tcp reverse shells) or base64. Check whether the node process was serving/rendering .prompty content. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the node PID, and block outbound to any IP the spawned shell contacted; snapshot for IR. 3) Hunt: confirm @prompty/core version on the host (UC2), review recently written/downloaded .prompty files for constructor/prototype/child_process tokens, and pivot on the same node process for further shell spawns or network egress.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment

Response runbook

Velocity.js SSTI RCE payload (constructor.constructor→child_process) in HTTP request AIDelivery High
Hunts inbound HTTP requests carrying the velocityjs template-injection RCE payload described in GHSA-7gfh-x38p-prh3: the property-read chain to the Function constructor plus a child_process command exec. Targets apps that render attacker-controlled Velocity templates (velocityjs ≤2.1.6) at the web/delivery layer where the payload is still visible on the wire. Rationale: Uses the exact exploit-chain strings from the PoC (constructor.constructor, process.mainModule, child_process, #set( / URL-encoded %23set() which are not present in normal Velocity rendering, giving high specificity at the delivery layer. Cross-checked against the prior fix advisory GHSA-j658-c2gf-x6pq (CVE-2026-44966) and the canonical JS-SSTI payload shape in PayloadsAllTheThings. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-j658-c2gf-x6pq • https://github.com/swisskyrepo/PayloadsAllTheThings/blob/master/Server%20Side%20Template%20Injection/JavaScript.md • https://github.com/shepherdwind/velocity.js/pull/192
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized vulnerability scanners (Nessus/Qualys/Nuclei) firing SSTI templates against the app
  • Contracted penetration test or bug-bounty researcher probing template injection
  • Internal AppSec CI/CD DAST scans (Burp/ZAP) hitting the endpoint
  • A benign field/parameter legitimately containing the literal string 'child_process' (e.g. a code-snippet paste or docs form) with no Velocity markers
  • Security awareness/red-team exercises replaying known SSTI payloads
Suppression filter
| where not(csUserAgent has_any ("Nessus","Qualys","Nuclei","Burp","ZAP","Nikto"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full request (URI, body, User-Agent, source IP) and confirm the payload contains a Velocity marker (#set / %23set) alongside constructor.constructor or child_process — a raw 'child_process' with no template syntax is likely benign. Check scStatus: a 200/500 suggests the template actually rendered. 2) Contain: if source IP is not an approved scanner, block it at the WAF/edge and confirm the app's velocityjs version — upgrade to 2.1.7+ immediately if ≤2.1.6. 3) Hunt: pivot to the same host's endpoint telemetry (UC 'node process spawning shell') for the ~5 min after the request to confirm code execution; review inbound requests from the same IP for other SSTI variants and check for outbound egress from the node process.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| extend Req = strcat(csUriStem, "?", csUriQuery)
| where Req contains "constructor.constructor" or Req contains "child_process" or Req contains "process.mainModule" or Req contains "mainModule" or Req contains "%23set(" or Req contains "#set("
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, sSiteName, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, csUserAgent, scStatus, csUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Node.js process spawning shell / recon binary (velocityjs SSTI RCE execution) AIExploitation High
Hunts the post-exploitation result of the velocityjs RCE: a node process (node.exe / node) spawning a command shell or reconnaissance binary via child_process.execSync, as in the PoC's execSync('whoami'). A production Node web server rarely shells out to cmd/powershell/sh + whoami, making the parent→child pair high-signal. Rationale: Anchors on the PoC's execution shape — node → child_process.execSync → shell/whoami — using node(.exe) as parent, which a running web server almost never legitimately does for interactive recon binaries. Covers both the article's verified Windows platform and the more common Linux Node deployment. Corroborated by the JS-SSTI child_process technique in PayloadsAllTheThings. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-7gfh-x38p-prh3 • https://github.com/swisskyrepo/PayloadsAllTheThings/blob/master/Server%20Side%20Template%20Injection/JavaScript.md
ATT&CKT1059.003 T1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Build/tooling: node running node-gyp, webpack, or npm lifecycle scripts that shell out to cmd/sh
  • Application features that legitimately call child_process (e.g. invoking git, ffmpeg, ImageMagick, or curl) as part of normal workflow
  • Process managers (PM2, forever) or health-check scripts spawning shells
  • DevOps CI runners executing node-based build steps that call whoami/hostname for diagnostics
  • Serverless/container entrypoints where node bootstraps a shell wrapper
Suppression filter
| where not(ProcessCommandLine has_any ("node-gyp","npm","npx","webpack","prebuild","install","git ","\\git.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: examine ProcessCommandLine of the child and InitiatingProcessCommandLine of node — a one-off `cmd /c whoami` / `sh -c whoami` with no build context is suspicious, whereas node-gyp/npm/git command lines are likely benign. Confirm the host runs a public-facing app using velocityjs ≤2.1.6. 2) Contain: isolate the host if the shell ran recon (whoami/ipconfig/net) with no legitimate parent chain; capture the node process command line and open sockets, then rotate any credentials/cloud tokens reachable from the app's environment. 3) Hunt: correlate to the web-layer UC for an inbound constructor.constructor/#set payload just prior; review the node process for subsequent outbound connections (reverse shell / staging), new file writes, and lateral movement from the host.

Vulnerable seroval (<=1.5.2) / TanStack Start (<1.167.30) exposure to CVE-2026-59940 deserialization RCE AIExploitation Medium
Hunts managed assets whose software inventory reports seroval prior to 1.5.3 or @tanstack/start-server-core prior to 1.167.30, the versions vulnerable to the fromJSON() Promise-resolver type-confusion that yields attacker-controlled server-side invocation. The exploit itself is deliberately non-observable in EDR (no PoC published, bypasses request middleware/audit logging and spawns no distinctive child process), so exposure hunting on the concrete package+version is the only reliable article-specific control. Rationale: Uses the exact concrete artifacts the advisory and corroborating sources fix: package `seroval` patched at 1.5.3 (vuln <=1.5.2), downstream `@tanstack/start-server-core` patched at 1.167.30, and CVE-2026-59940. No runtime IOC is emitted because the disclosure explicitly omits the PoC and the exploit bypasses middleware/audit logging with no distinctive process artifact — so exposure hunting is the only article-specific, executable detection. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-mv8w-475r-vwqw • https://cvereports.com/reports/GHSA-9M65-766C-R333 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-SEROVAL-15054506
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.007
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer/CI/build hosts carrying seroval as a transitive dependency but not running an internet-facing TanStack server function endpoint
  • Assets already remediated but showing stale MDVM inventory before the next scan refresh
  • Bundled/minified copies where the version string is inaccurate or absent, causing over- or under-matching on parse_version
  • Non-server workstations that have the package via an Electron/desktop app with no untrusted-client Seroval JSON ingress
  • Name-collision matches on unrelated software containing the token 'tanstack' or 'seroval' in the display name
Suppression filter
| where not (SoftwareName has "seroval" and parse_version(SoftwareVersion) >= parse_version("1.5.3")) and not (SoftwareName has "tanstack" and parse_version(SoftwareVersion) >= parse_version("1.167.30"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reported SoftwareVersion is genuinely below the fix (seroval 1.5.3 / start-server-core 1.167.30) and check DeviceInfo.IsInternetFacing plus whether the host actually serves TanStack server functions that deserialize client JSON. 2) Contain: prioritise internet-facing hosts — upgrade to the patched versions and, as defense-in-depth, restrict accepted Seroval node types and allowlist plugin tags for inbound deserialization; isolate any endpoint that cannot be patched immediately. 3) Hunt: on confirmed-exposed hosts review app/WAF logs for POSTs to server-function routes with anomalously large or structurally unusual JSON bodies, and look for any node process spawning unexpected children as a post-deserialization side effect.

`kiota info` run against a remote/untrusted OpenAPI description (CVE-2026-59865) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces `kiota info` invocations that read an OpenAPI descriptor from a remote/untrusted HTTP(S) source — the precondition for CVE-2026-59865, where a spec-supplied `x-ms-kiota-info.dependencyInstallCommand` replaces kiota's trusted install suggestion and can drive `curl | bash`-style RCE on the developer/CI host. Hunts the kiota process itself with `info` plus a remote `-d`/`--openapi` descriptor rather than a shell child (which the developer or VS Code extension, not kiota, executes). Rationale: Anchors on the article's concrete invocation surface — the `kiota info` subcommand plus a remote `-d`/`--openapi` descriptor — which is the exact vulnerable path (`x-ms-kiota-info.dependencyInstallCommand` is only read/surfaced by `kiota info`). Avoids the unreliable spec-URL field (no such EDR column) and instead pivots on the kiota process image + command line. Cross-checked the GHSA advisory (CVSS 9.3, fixed 1.32.5, PR #7883) and the sibling Kiota CVE-2026-41134 advisory to confirm behaviour and affected packages. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hq9q-27g5-qwpj • https://github.com/microsoft/kiota/pull/7883 • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-2hx3-vp6r-mg3f
ATT&CKT1059 T1195.001 T1204
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately running `kiota info -d https://<trusted>/openapi.json` against a first-party/internal API spec
  • CI/CD client-generation pipelines that fetch a well-known remote descriptor (e.g. petstore.swagger.io) on every build
  • Platform teams evaluating third-party public APIs where the remote OpenAPI URL is expected
  • kiota invoked via `dotnet kiota` wrapper where the parent, not FileName, carries the 'kiota' token
  • Automated doc/SDK-refresh jobs pulling a partner's hosted OpenAPI description
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("petstore.swagger.io", "raw.githubusercontent.com/microsoft", "://localhost", "://127.0.0.1"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and resolve the `-d`/`--openapi` URL — is the host untrusted/newly-seen? Confirm the local kiota/VS Code extension version is < 1.32.5 (vulnerable). 2) Contain: on the same host/user within the next few minutes, hunt for a shell running the surfaced install command (`curl`/`wget` piped to `bash`/`sh`, `Invoke-WebRequest`|`IEX`, or `rm -rf`); if found, isolate the workstation/CI runner and reset any dev/cloud/registry credentials reachable from it. 3) Hunt: enumerate all hosts that ran `kiota info` against that URL, block the descriptor domain and any downloaded payload domain, and push the 1.32.5+ upgrade to kiota and the VS Code extension fleet-wide.

Microsoft Kiota APIPlugin manifest generation from OpenAPI spec (CVE-2026-59864) AIWeaponization Medium
Hunts execution of `kiota plugin add` / `kiota plugin generate -t APIPlugin`, the propagation point where an attacker-controlled OpenAPI description's x-ai-adaptive-card / x-ai-capabilities extensions are written verbatim into `<name>-apiplugin.json`. Surfaces the OpenAPI source and kiota version so analysts can confirm whether a poisoned static_template.file path (../ or absolute) was minted. Rationale: Anchors on the article's exact CLI verbs (`plugin add`/`plugin generate`) plus the `APIPlugin` template type and the `kiota` binary (confirmed on the self-contained linux-x64 1.32.4 release; also runs as kiota.exe / `dotnet kiota` on Windows dev hosts). This is the one host-observable event in the chain — where the poisoned static_template.file is emitted. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-4jwf-m4wg-8p66 • https://github.com/microsoft/kiota/pull/7892

Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment

  • CI/CD pipeline auto-regenerating plugin manifests on each build
  • Engineer following Kiota plugin tutorials/PoC testing
  • Platform team standardising internal API plugins from a vetted spec registry
